AI Development English

For AI engineers, researchers, product managers, data specialists, safety teams, and AI-adjacent leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Distinguish components and ask a diagnostic question without assigning blame.
  • Correct a technical misconception tactfully and negotiate a bounded alternative.
  • Disclose compromised evidence and resist pressure to overstate readiness.
  • Negotiate a trade-off using matched comparisons and explicitly limited claims.
  • Separate proposed remedies by mechanism and agree on a controlled experiment.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Speaking the AI Development StackThe answer is wrong, but which layer failed?+

Your goal

Distinguish components and ask a diagnostic question without assigning blame.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The answer is wrong, but which layer failed?

A document assistant displays the wrong refund deadline. Retrieval returned the current policy, which states 30 days, but the generated answer says 60. The interface displays the generated text unchanged. Priya owns the model integration; Alex manages the product. A demonstration is scheduled for Friday. No cause has been confirmed, and Alex cannot approve a production release alone.

Priya: AI integration engineer / Alex: Product manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which observation is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

B. The generated answer says 60 days.

The briefing identifies the generated text as wrong and says the interface displays it unchanged.

2. Who owns the model integration?

Answer and explanation

B. Priya

Priya's stated role covers integration; product ownership does not identify the technical owner.

3. What remains unknown?

Answer and explanation

C. Why the model produced the wrong deadline

The symptom and correct deadline are known, but the cause has not been established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A trace records events; a diagnosis explains them. A wrong answer identifies a symptom, not automatically a model defect. Keep retrieval output, model output, and displayed text separate.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
application stack
The components that together deliver an application.map the application stack
retrieval
Finding source material relevant to a request.inspect retrieval results
language model
A model that processes and generates language sequences.query a language model
user interface
The part of a system a user sees and operates.inspect the user interface
orchestration
Coordination of model, retrieval, and tool steps.trace the orchestration flow
inference
Running a trained model to produce an output.run inference
endpoint
An address through which a service accepts requests.call an endpoint
payload
The data carried in a request or response.inspect the request payload
trace
A linked record of events in a request's path.examine a request trace
dependency
A component or condition another part relies on.identify a service dependency
upstream
Earlier in the relevant processing path.inspect the upstream response
downstream
Later in the relevant processing path.assess downstream impact
symptom
An observed sign of a problem, not its cause.describe the symptom
root cause
The underlying cause established by investigation.verify the root cause
reproduction
Recreating a reported result under stated conditions.obtain a reliable reproduction
request ID
An identifier used to locate a particular request.attach the request ID
schema
A defined structure for data and its fields.validate a response schema
tool call
A model-requested invocation of an available tool.inspect a tool call
API
Application programming interface; a defined software interaction boundary.document an API contract
contract
Agreed expectations between software components.preserve the service contract
integration
Connecting components into a working system.test the integration
observability
The ability to infer system behavior from available signals.improve observability
triage
Initial assessment that determines priority and ownership.triage the defect
handoff
Transfer of work and its relevant context.prepare a clear handoff

An API contract defines expected interaction, not necessarily a legal contract. "Upstream" and "downstream" describe position relative to a chosen component; name that component.

C Notice the language

Locate the failure before naming an owner

"The AI is broken" does not identify a component or establish responsibility. Separate the observed output from a theory about its cause. A colleague asking for the exact trace is asking for evidence, not necessarily dismissing the report.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the layer
Which component first shows the wrong value?
State the observation
The retrieved passage says 30 days; the generated answer says 60.
Limit the conclusion
That locates the mismatch, but it does not establish the cause.
Request a trace
Can you attach the request ID and the redacted trace?
Separate responsibilities
Product approval and technical diagnosis are different decisions.
Ask for reproduction
Can we reproduce it with the same payload and configuration?
Clarify a term
By "the AI," do you mean the model or the whole application?
Agree on a handoff
I will send the evidence and name the unresolved question.
Check the interface
Does the interface display the response without changing it?
Avoid blame
Let us inspect the boundary before assigning ownership.
Narrow the test
Change one component while keeping the others fixed.
Name the uncertainty
We have an observation, not a verified root cause.
Confirm a dependency
Does this test depend on the policy index being current?
Preserve the record
Please retain the configuration used for that request.
Set the update
I can commit to an update, not to a fix by that time.
Close precisely
The demonstration remains scheduled; release approval is separate.
6 language and precision notes
Which, not why
"Which component first shows the mismatch?" asks for location before assuming a cause.
Present evidence
"The trace shows" introduces an observation; "this suggests" introduces an interpretation.
Embedded questions
Say "Can you confirm whether the interface changes it?", not "whether does the interface change it?"
Contrast clauses
Use "retrieval returned the right passage, but generation changed the deadline" to isolate the contrast.
Limited commitments
"I will investigate by Friday" is unclear; "I will report our findings by Friday" names a deliverable.
Ownership language
"I own the investigation" accepts responsibility for the work, not blame for the incident.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question locates the first mismatch?

Answer and explanation

C. At which boundary does 30 become 60?

Comparing values across boundaries locates the observed mismatch; the alternatives jump to cause, responsibility, or resolution without the necessary evidence.

2. Complete: "Could you confirm whether ___?"

Answer and explanation

B. the payload includes the policy

After whether, an embedded question takes statement order: subject followed by verb.

3. Which statement preserves uncertainty?

Answer and explanation

A. The trace suggests a generation issue; the cause is unconfirmed.

Suggests reports limited evidence, while the second clause explicitly leaves causation unresolved.

4. Which expression means transferring work with context?

Answer and explanation

A. prepare a clear handoff

A handoff transfers work and relevant information; the other combinations do not express that action.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The answer is wrong, but which layer failed?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: AI integration engineer / Alex: Product manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • request ID
  • mismatch
  • payload
  • configuration
  • handoff
  • triage
  • upstream
  • trace
  • reproduction
  • root cause
  1. Alex
    The refund answer is wrong again. The policy says 30 days, but our assistant tells the customer 60. Which team should investigate first?
  2. Priya
    Do you have the 1? I want to see the retrieved passage and the answer side by side.
  3. Alex
    Yes, the record is D-184. I checked the passage in that record: it says 30 days. The response underneath it says 60.
  4. Priya
    That gives us a specific 2. Retrieval found the current passage. I still need to inspect exactly what reached the model.
  5. Alex
    So the model got it wrong? I need to tell the team who is picking this up.
  6. Priya
    Tell them the answer is wrong, but leave the cause open. The 3 could contain another instruction or an older example.
  7. Alex
    Wait, could the interface have changed the number? We had a display issue last time.
  8. Priya
    The recorded response already says 60, and the interface displays it unchanged. That puts the first observed difference 4 of presentation.
  9. Alex
    What would you need to distinguish a bad instruction from an unstable model answer? We should keep the Friday demonstration team informed.
  10. Priya
    The configuration and a redacted 5. Then I can repeat the request and compare the inputs without changing several variables.
  11. Alex
    Is repeating one request enough to say we have fixed the problem? The same customer asked two slightly different versions of the question.
  12. Priya
    No. First we need a reliable 6. Then we test the change on both variants and related policy cases.
  13. Alex
    Please make the ownership clear in the ticket. I can coordinate the demonstration, but I cannot approve production use on my own.
  14. Priya
    I will own technical 7. I will also name the policy and interface owners if the evidence requires their input.
  15. Alex
    Can I tell the sales team we will have a fix by Thursday afternoon? They need to decide whether to keep the live demonstration.
  16. Priya
    I can promise findings by then, not a fix. Until we verify a 8, that deadline would be an unsupported commitment.
  17. Alex
    All right. I will describe the customer-visible problem and keep the demonstration decision separate. What should your update include for that decision?
  18. Priya
    The reproduction result, affected cases, remaining uncertainty, and a proposed next step. I will preserve the 9 so the comparison is meaningful.
  19. Alex
    That works. Please send it by three on Thursday, even if the investigation is incomplete. I will confirm who can decide about Friday.
  20. Priya
    Agreed. The 10 will say what we know, what we do not know, and which decision still needs an owner.

Answers and explanations
  1. request ID Turn 2. A request ID identifies the particular request whose recorded behavior can be inspected.
  2. mismatch Turn 4. A mismatch is a disagreement between values that should agree; here the two deadlines differ.
  3. payload Turn 6. The payload is the data sent in the request; it may differ from material displayed elsewhere.
  4. upstream Turn 8. Upstream places the observed difference before the interface in this processing path.
  5. trace Turn 10. A trace links events along the request path and helps compare what each component received or returned.
  6. reproduction Turn 12. A reproduction recreates the observed problem; it is not proof that a later change solves every variation.
  7. triage Turn 14. Triage is the initial assessment of the defect and its appropriate handling, not final release approval.
  8. root cause Turn 16. A root cause is an established underlying explanation, not merely the first plausible theory.
  9. configuration Turn 18. The configuration records the settings used, allowing results from different runs to be compared meaningfully.
  10. handoff Turn 20. A handoff passes findings and responsibility to the next decision-maker without implying the investigation is complete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: The refund answer is wrong again. The policy says 30 days, but our assistant tells the customer 60. Which team should investigate first?
  2. Priya: Do you have the request ID? I want to see the retrieved passage and the answer side by side.
  3. Alex: Yes, the record is D-184. I checked the passage in that record: it says 30 days. The response underneath it says 60.
  4. Priya: That gives us a specific mismatch. Retrieval found the current passage. I still need to inspect exactly what reached the model.
  5. Alex: So the model got it wrong? I need to tell the team who is picking this up.
  6. Priya: Tell them the answer is wrong, but leave the cause open. The payload could contain another instruction or an older example.
  7. Alex: Wait, could the interface have changed the number? We had a display issue last time.
  8. Priya: The recorded response already says 60, and the interface displays it unchanged. That puts the first observed difference upstream of presentation.
  9. Alex: What would you need to distinguish a bad instruction from an unstable model answer? We should keep the Friday demonstration team informed.
  10. Priya: The configuration and a redacted trace. Then I can repeat the request and compare the inputs without changing several variables.
  11. Alex: Is repeating one request enough to say we have fixed the problem? The same customer asked two slightly different versions of the question.
  12. Priya: No. First we need a reliable reproduction. Then we test the change on both variants and related policy cases.
  13. Alex: Please make the ownership clear in the ticket. I can coordinate the demonstration, but I cannot approve production use on my own.
  14. Priya: I will own technical triage. I will also name the policy and interface owners if the evidence requires their input.
  15. Alex: Can I tell the sales team we will have a fix by Thursday afternoon? They need to decide whether to keep the live demonstration.
  16. Priya: I can promise findings by then, not a fix. Until we verify a root cause, that deadline would be an unsupported commitment.
  17. Alex: All right. I will describe the customer-visible problem and keep the demonstration decision separate. What should your update include for that decision?
  18. Priya: The reproduction result, affected cases, remaining uncertainty, and a proposed next step. I will preserve the configuration so the comparison is meaningful.
  19. Alex: That works. Please send it by three on Thursday, even if the investigation is incomplete. I will confirm who can decide about Friday.
  20. Priya: Agreed. The handoff will say what we know, what we do not know, and which decision still needs an owner.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which component is failing?

An engineer and a product manager inspect an incorrect answer from a document assistant.

  1. Product manager: The AI gave the wrong answer. Should we replace the model?
  2. Engineer: First, let's separate retrieval, answer generation, and what the interface displays.
  3. Product manager: The displayed answer refers to last year's handbook. Which layer controls that?
  4. Engineer: Retrieval supplies passages. The language model uses them to draft an answer.
  5. Product manager: So an old passage could produce an answer that sounds current?
  6. Engineer: Yes. We need the retrieved passages before attributing this example to the model.
  7. Product manager: The trace shows last year's handbook among the returned documents.
  8. Engineer: Then let's inspect document versioning and retrieval before changing the foundation model.
  9. Product manager: I'll attach the question, displayed answer, and document version to the issue.
  10. Engineer: Good. I'll trace that request and distinguish confirmed findings from possible causes.
A queue is not a slow model

A serving engineer explains response timing to a new colleague.

  1. Colleague: Users waited twelve seconds, but the inference log records only four.
  2. Serving engineer: The request also spent eight seconds waiting in the serving queue.
  3. Colleague: Does inference mean the entire period from clicking Send to seeing an answer?
  4. Serving engineer: Here, inference means running the model. End-to-end latency includes the other stages.
  5. Colleague: Then our dashboard label is making two different measures look interchangeable.
  6. Serving engineer: Exactly. Let's label queue time, model execution, and total response time separately.
  7. Colleague: Would increasing throughput mean every individual request becomes faster?
  8. Serving engineer: Not necessarily. Throughput measures completed work per period, not one request's duration.
  9. Colleague: I'll revise the labels and include both measures in the incident summary.
  10. Serving engineer: Include the measurement boundaries too, so the next comparison uses the same definition.
One answer, two displays

A frontend developer and an AI engineer investigate a missing citation.

  1. Frontend developer: The answer on screen has no citation, so retrieval must have failed.
  2. AI engineer: Let's check the response payload before drawing that conclusion.
  3. Frontend developer: The payload contains a passage identifier and a citation URL.
  4. AI engineer: Then the backend returned citation information. The display may be dropping it.
  5. Frontend developer: The new interface expects a citations array, but this response uses source.
  6. AI engineer: That looks like a contract mismatch between the service and the interface.
  7. Frontend developer: I'll reproduce it with a saved response rather than another live model call.
  8. AI engineer: Good. That isolates rendering from retrieval and generation variability.
  9. Frontend developer: Once the mapping is fixed, should we also test empty citation lists?
  10. AI engineer: Yes. Check both cases and avoid treating missing evidence as a display-only problem.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The answer is wrong, but which layer failed?
  1. Check the dialogue, then read the 30-day source and 60-day generated answer as separate observations. Locate the mismatch before assigning its cause.
  2. Switch roles. Ask for D-184, the redacted trace and the request configuration; retain the Thursday findings update without promising a fix.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A missing citation in a different assistant

A support assistant generates a correct answer with a citation ID. The interface omits the citation link. The display team needs the exact recorded request, not an assumption that retrieval failed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Engineer: "The model response contains the citation; the ___ does not display it."

Answer and explanation

B. user interface

The discrepancy appears in presentation because the generated response already contains the citation.

2. Lead: "Attach the ___ so the display team can locate that exchange."

Answer and explanation

B. request ID

The identifier locates the particular exchange rather than merely describing the application.

3. Engineer: "That is the observed ___; we have not proved why it occurred."

Answer and explanation

D. symptom

An omitted link is an observed sign; its underlying cause still needs investigation.

4. Lead: "Then send a clear ___ to the display owner."

Answer and explanation

A. handoff

The next team needs the evidence and unresolved question to take over the investigation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Conversation explanations in the book, page 86 ↗

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Professional audience: AI engineers, researchers, product managers, data specialists, safety teams, and AI-adjacent leaders
Scope: Fictional communication practice, not deployment, security, or compliance instructions. Use authorized review, current documentation, and approved procedures for real systems. Never place real secrets or personal data in practice exercises.
Lesson 01: Speaking the AI Development Stack
Original fictional case: A document assistant displays the wrong refund deadline. Retrieval returned the current policy, which states 30 days, but the generated answer says 60. The interface displays the generated text unchanged. Priya owns the model integration; Alex manages the product. A demonstration is scheduled for Friday. No cause has been confirmed, and Alex cannot approve a production release alone.
Language workshop: The answer is wrong, but which layer failed?
Communication goal: Distinguish components and ask a diagnostic question without assigning blame.
Language guidance: A trace records events; a diagnosis explains them. A wrong answer identifies a symptom, not automatically a model defect. Keep retrieval output, model output, and displayed text separate.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which component first shows the wrong value? / The retrieved passage says 30 days; the generated answer says 60. / That locates the mismatch, but it does not establish the cause. / Can you attach the request ID and the redacted trace? / Product approval and technical diagnosis are different decisions. / Can we reproduce it with the same payload and configuration? / By "the AI," do you mean the model or the whole application? / I will send the evidence and name the unresolved question. / Does the interface display the response without changing it? / Let us inspect the boundary before assigning ownership. / Change one component while keeping the others fixed. / We have an observation, not a verified root cause. / Does this test depend on the policy index being current? / Please retain the configuration used for that request. / I can commit to an update, not to a fix by that time. / The demonstration remains scheduled; release approval is separate.
Vocabulary: application stack: The components that together deliver an application. / retrieval: Finding source material relevant to a request. / language model: A model that processes and generates language sequences. / user interface: The part of a system a user sees and operates. / orchestration: Coordination of model, retrieval, and tool steps. / inference: Running a trained model to produce an output. / endpoint: An address through which a service accepts requests. / payload: The data carried in a request or response. / trace: A linked record of events in a request's path. / dependency: A component or condition another part relies on. / upstream: Earlier in the relevant processing path. / downstream: Later in the relevant processing path. / symptom: An observed sign of a problem, not its cause. / root cause: The underlying cause established by investigation. / reproduction: Recreating a reported result under stated conditions. / request ID: An identifier used to locate a particular request. / schema: A defined structure for data and its fields. / tool call: A model-requested invocation of an available tool. / API: Application programming interface; a defined software interaction boundary. / contract: Agreed expectations between software components. / integration: Connecting components into a working system. / observability: The ability to infer system behavior from available signals. / triage: Initial assessment that determines priority and ownership. / handoff: Transfer of work and its relevant context.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue, then read the 30-day source and 60-day generated answer as separate observations. Locate the mismatch before assigning its cause. Switch roles. Ask for D-184, the redacted trace and the request configuration; retain the Thursday findings update without promising a fix. Complete the citation-link exchange. State that the generated response contains the citation while the interface omits it, then hand over the exact request.
Partner: Priya: AI integration engineer / Alex: Product manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A support assistant generates a correct answer with a citation ID. The interface omits the citation link. The display team needs the exact recorded request, not an assumption that retrieval failed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: All right. I will describe the customer-visible problem and keep the demonstration decision separate. What should your update include for that decision?
Priya: The reproduction result, affected cases, remaining uncertainty, and a proposed next step. I will preserve the configuration so the comparison is meaningful.
Alex: That works. Please send it by three on Thursday, even if the investigation is incomplete. I will confirm who can decide about Friday.
Priya: Agreed. The handoff will say what we know, what we do not know, and which decision still needs an owner.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Vocabulary: application stack: The components that together deliver an application. / retrieval: Finding source material relevant to a request. / language model: A model that processes and generates language sequences. / user interface: The part of a system a user sees and operates. / orchestration: Coordination of model, retrieval, and tool steps. / inference: Running a trained model to produce an output. / endpoint: An address through which a service accepts requests. / payload: The data carried in a request or response. / trace: A linked record of events in a request's path. / dependency: A component or condition another part relies on. / upstream: Earlier in the relevant processing path. / downstream: Later in the relevant processing path. / symptom: An observed sign of a problem, not its cause. / root cause: The underlying cause established by investigation. / reproduction: Recreating a reported result under stated conditions. / request ID: An identifier used to locate a particular request. / schema: A defined structure for data and its fields. / tool call: A model-requested invocation of an available tool. / API: Application programming interface; a defined software interaction boundary. / contract: Agreed expectations between software components. / integration: Connecting components into a working system. / observability: The ability to infer system behavior from available signals. / triage: Initial assessment that determines priority and ownership. / handoff: Transfer of work and its relevant context.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue, then read the 30-day source and 60-day generated answer as separate observations. Locate the mismatch before assigning its cause. Switch roles. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. 02LLMs, Transformers, Tokens, and ContextA larger answer will not make the input fit+

Your goal

Correct a technical misconception tactfully and negotiate a bounded alternative.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A larger answer will not make the input fit

A document-summary request reserves 4,000 output tokens. Its instructions and documents contain 29,000 input tokens. The configured total context limit is 32,000 tokens, and the request is rejected before generation. Lina proposes increasing the output allowance. Omar owns the request configuration. The team must retain the exception clauses; no larger-context model has been evaluated for this task.

Lina: Application engineer / Omar: Model platform engineer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the total requested reservation?

Answer and explanation

D. 33,000 tokens

The input contains 29,000 tokens and the output reservation adds 4,000, giving 33,000.

2. Why was the request rejected?

Answer and explanation

C. It exceeds the configured total context limit.

Rejection happened before generation because the combined reservation exceeds 32,000 tokens.

3. What must the team preserve?

Answer and explanation

C. The exception clauses

The briefing requires the exception clauses to remain available; it does not require duplicate material.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A token is not a word. Input size, reserved output, and total context are different quantities. The numerical limits in this case are fictional configuration facts, not specifications for a named model.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
LLM
Large language model; a model that processes and generates language.evaluate an LLM
transformer
A neural architecture built around attention mechanisms.use a transformer architecture
token
A unit processed by a model, not necessarily a whole word.count input tokens
tokenizer
The component that converts text into tokens.use the model's tokenizer
context window
The amount of context a model can handle in a request.stay within the context window
input tokens
Tokens supplied to the model before generation.measure input tokens
output tokens
Tokens generated by the model.reserve output tokens
token budget
An allocated number of tokens for a task or component.allocate a token budget
system instruction
Higher-priority guidance supplied by an application's configuration.inspect the system instruction
prompt
Instructions and other context supplied for a model response.revise the prompt
attention
A mechanism that weights relationships between representations.compute attention weights
parameter
A learned numerical value within a model.update model parameters
weight
A learned numerical coefficient in a model.load model weights
checkpoint
Saved model state from a particular stage.select a checkpoint
truncation
Removal of content to fit a size limit.detect silent truncation
compression
Reduction of information size, potentially losing detail.assess compression losses
reserved capacity
Capacity set aside rather than already consumed.reduce reserved capacity
delimiter
A marker separating sections of supplied text.preserve clear delimiters
instruction hierarchy
Priority relationships among different instruction sources.respect the instruction hierarchy
long-context
Able to handle relatively large input context.evaluate long-context performance
position
A token's place within the input sequence.vary evidence position
exception clause
Wording that limits or changes a general rule.retain the exception clause
deduplication
Removal of repeated copies of content.apply exact deduplication
headroom
Unused capacity left below a limit.leave sufficient headroom

Deduplication removes repeated material; summarization changes its representation. Neither guarantees that every important exception remains visible. A larger context window does not by itself establish task accuracy.

C Notice the language

Correct the model of the problem

Restate the proposal before rejecting it. Explain which limit applies and show the arithmetic. "That would increase the reservation" is more useful than "You do not understand tokens." A correction can be direct without judging the colleague.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Restate a proposal
You are proposing a larger output allowance, correct?
Locate the limit
The request fails before generation because the combined reservation is too large.
Show the arithmetic
Twenty-nine thousand plus four thousand is thirty-three thousand.
Correct tactfully
I see the aim, but that change would increase the reservation.
Distinguish quantities
The allowance is a maximum, not the length already generated.
Preserve a requirement
We must retain the exception clauses in the source context.
Offer an alternative
Could we remove exact duplicates before shortening substantive text?
State a condition
A smaller output allowance is acceptable only if the summary still meets the task.
Check counting
Are those counts from the tokenizer used by this model?
Avoid a guarantee
Fitting within the limit does not prove the answer is complete.
Check a transformation
Which information would compression remove?
Reject silent loss
Please report truncation instead of silently dropping the end.
Bound a comparison
Let us compare the same documents under both configurations.
Keep a fallback
If the exceptions cannot fit, route the request for a different workflow.
Separate evaluation
The larger-context model needs its own quality check.
Read back the decision
We will remove duplicates, recount, and test coverage before changing models.
6 language and precision notes
Arithmetic in speech
Say "thirty-three thousand in total" after stating the two parts; listeners can check the sum.
Would versus will
"That would increase the reservation" describes the proposed change without claiming it has happened.
Only if
"Acceptable only if coverage is preserved" makes coverage a necessary condition.
Not necessarily
"A larger window is not necessarily more accurate" rejects a guarantee without claiming it is always worse.
Scope of all
"All exceptions in these documents" is bounded; "all exceptions" can imply knowledge beyond the supplied material.
Clarifying by
"By output allowance, do you mean the maximum reserved tokens?" checks a term before challenging it.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response explains the rejection?

Answer and explanation

A. The combined reservation is 1,000 tokens over the limit.

The request totals 33,000 against a 32,000 limit; generation has not begun.

2. Which proposal preserves the stated requirement most directly?

Answer and explanation

A. Remove exact duplicate headers and recount.

Removing exact duplicates can reduce size without intentionally deleting the required exceptions; the result still needs checking.

3. Complete: "We can reduce the allowance, provided that ___."

Answer and explanation

C. the summary still covers the required points

Provided that introduces a condition with normal subject-verb order.

4. Which claim is appropriately limited?

Answer and explanation

B. The request fits; we still need to test summary coverage.

Capacity compliance and content quality are separate checks; fitting does not prove coverage.

  4. Omar
    For this configuration, yes. The combined 2 is 33,000. Increasing the output allowance would move us further over the limit.
  5. Lina

  6. Omar
    Not without checking what it contains. The final pages include an 3 that changes the refund rule for renewals.
  7. Lina
    We also repeat the same header on every page. Removing those exact copies would preserve the policy wording and reduce the input somewhat.
  8. Omar
    That is a reasonable first test. Use exact 4, then count again with the tokenizer used by this model rather than a word count.
  9. Lina
    Suppose that saves only 600 tokens. We would still be 400 over the limit. Could we reserve 3,000 output tokens instead?
  10. Omar
    We could test it. That would leave 600 tokens of 5, but we need to verify that the shorter answer still covers the required points.
  11. Lina
    I'll check renewals and exceptions against the same checklist. Getting the request accepted won't help if the summary drops the important part.
  12. Omar
    Exactly. Also record whether the client performs 6. A request can fit after important text has been removed without anyone noticing.
  13. Lina
    Someone suggested using a larger-context model instead. Would that let us keep the original request and stop spending time on these smaller adjustments?

  16. Omar
    Vary its 8 while keeping the wording unchanged. We want to see whether the model preserves the exception across the tested arrangements.
  17. Lina
    I'll remove the repeated headers first. If we're still over, I'll test the smaller output allowance and bring both the counts and the coverage results.
  18. Omar
    Correct. Keep the 9 between documents so the revised input does not merge separate policies into one undifferentiated block.
  19. Lina
    I will report the before-and-after counts with the coverage results. If the necessary clauses still cannot fit, I will stop rather than silently remove them.
  20. Omar
    Good. Describe the limit as a 10, not a model-quality finding. We can then choose the next workflow using the actual test results.

Answers and explanations
  1. token budget Turn 2. A token budget allocates capacity to the request; checking it reveals whether the proposed change fits.
  2. reservation Turn 4. The reservation combines supplied input and capacity set aside for output under this case's stated rule.
  3. exception clause Turn 6. An exception clause changes the general rule, so deleting it can change the meaning of the policy.
  4. deduplication Turn 8. Deduplication removes repeated copies; it is distinct from summarizing or deleting unique policy content.
  5. headroom Turn 10. Headroom is the remaining capacity below the limit: 32,000 minus 28,400 minus 3,000 equals 600.
  6. truncation Turn 12. Truncation removes content to satisfy a length limit; recording it exposes possible information loss.
  7. long-context Turn 14. Long-context describes capacity for larger inputs, not a guarantee that relevant details will be used accurately.
  8. position Turn 16. Position means where the evidence appears in the input; changing it can reveal sensitivity to document order.
  9. delimiters Turn 18. Delimiters mark document or section boundaries; retaining them helps preserve the structure of the supplied context.
  10. capacity constraint Turn 20. A capacity constraint limits what fits; it is not a finding about the truth or quality of the policy answer.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Lina: The summary request is rejected again. I was going to increase the output allowance so the model has enough room to explain the policy.
  2. Omar: Can we check the token budget first? Your input is 29,000 tokens, and you have reserved another 4,000 for output.
  3. Lina: That sounds close to the 32,000 limit. Are you saying the unused output allowance counts even though the model has not answered yet?
  4. Omar: For this configuration, yes. The combined reservation is 33,000. Increasing the output allowance would move us further over the limit.
  5. Lina: All right, I had treated the limit as input only. Could we simply trim the final document until the request is accepted?
  6. Omar: Not without checking what it contains. The final pages include an exception clause that changes the refund rule for renewals.
  7. Lina: We also repeat the same header on every page. Removing those exact copies would preserve the policy wording and reduce the input somewhat.
  8. Omar: That is a reasonable first test. Use exact deduplication, then count again with the tokenizer used by this model rather than a word count.
  9. Lina: Suppose that saves only 600 tokens. We would still be 400 over the limit. Could we reserve 3,000 output tokens instead?
  10. Omar: We could test it. That would leave 600 tokens of headroom, but we need to verify that the shorter answer still covers the required points.
  11. Lina: I'll check renewals and exceptions against the same checklist. Getting the request accepted won't help if the summary drops the important part.
  12. Omar: Exactly. Also record whether the client performs truncation. A request can fit after important text has been removed without anyone noticing.
  13. Lina: Someone suggested using a larger-context model instead. Would that let us keep the original request and stop spending time on these smaller adjustments?

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

More output space will not fit the input

Two engineers review a request rejected for exceeding the model's context limit.

  1. Developer: The request is too long. Could we just increase the output allowance?
  2. AI engineer: That won't shorten the prompt or the retrieved documents already in the request.
  3. Developer: I thought the context window meant the answer's maximum length.
  4. AI engineer: It is the model's available context, not simply the number of answer tokens.
  5. Developer: Our application reserves answer space before adding retrieved passages.
  6. AI engineer: Then we should inspect that budget and count the assembled request.
  7. Developer: Could I delete the oldest conversation messages until it fits?
  8. AI engineer: Only after checking whether they contain constraints the current question still needs.
  9. Developer: I'll measure each part and propose a documented truncation rule.
  10. AI engineer: Test that rule on follow-up questions, not only standalone prompts.
The token count changed

A localization lead compares English and Japanese requests in the team's test environment.

  1. Localization lead: Both requests have about the same character count. Why do their token counts differ?
  2. Engineer: The tokenizer doesn't assign exactly one token to each character or word.
  3. Localization lead: Can I use the English average to estimate the Japanese requests?
  4. Engineer: We should measure those requests with the tokenizer used by our deployed model.
  5. Localization lead: The Japanese sample includes product codes and mixed punctuation as well.
  6. Engineer: Include those in the test set. They are part of our actual input.
  7. Localization lead: Would a word limit in the interface still protect the context budget?
  8. Engineer: It would be an approximation, not a guarantee that the assembled request fits.
  9. Localization lead: I'll collect representative samples and flag the largest assembled requests.
  10. Engineer: I'll report token counts by sample and keep the tokenizer version in the results.
Instructions and quoted text

A prompt designer and reviewer discuss a document summarization request.

  1. Reviewer: This quoted document tells the assistant to ignore the requested output format.
  2. Prompt designer: That sentence is document content, not an instruction we intend to authorize.
  3. Reviewer: Is placing our format rule in the system prompt sufficient proof of protection?
  4. Prompt designer: No. We still need to test whether the assistant follows the intended boundary.
  5. Reviewer: Let's include documents that contain instruction-like language in the evaluation.
  6. Prompt designer: Agreed. I'll keep document text clearly delimited in the request structure.
  7. Reviewer: Should the summary mention the instruction-like sentence if it is relevant?
  8. Prompt designer: It may describe the sentence as content without treating it as a command.
  9. Reviewer: I'll check both the output format and whether any unauthorized action occurred.
  10. Prompt designer: Record the full test conditions so a passing example doesn't become a blanket claim.

A larger answer will not make the input fit
  1. After checking the dialogue, read the reservation calculation aloud: 29,000 input plus 4,000 reserved output equals 33,000 against a 32,000 limit.
  2. Switch roles. Read the proposal to remove exact duplicates while retaining the exception clauses; do not treat a fitting request as proof of coverage.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A contract summary with repeated appendices

A fictional request has a 20,000-token total limit, 18,000 input tokens, and a 3,000-token output reservation. Repeated appendix covers may be removed; unique obligations must remain.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Engineer: "The combined ___ is 21,000 tokens, so the request is too large."

Answer and explanation

C. reservation

Adding 18,000 input and 3,000 reserved output gives 21,000, above the case limit.

2. Reviewer: "Start with exact ___ of the repeated covers."

Answer and explanation

D. deduplication

Removing exact repeated covers targets redundancy without intentionally removing unique obligations.

3. Engineer: "I will preserve every unique ___ in the supplied contract."

Answer and explanation

A. obligation

The constraint explicitly protects unique obligations; removing them would change the source information.

4. Reviewer: "After recounting, test ___ rather than treating acceptance as success."

Answer and explanation

D. coverage

The system accepting the request does not establish that the summary includes the required obligations.

Lesson 02: LLMs, Transformers, Tokens, and Context

The configured total context limit is 32,000 tokens, and the request is rejected before generation. Lina proposes increasing the output allowance. Omar owns the request configuration. The team must retain the exception clauses; no larger-context model has been evaluated for this task.
Language workshop: A larger answer will not make the input fit
Communication goal: Correct a technical misconception tactfully and negotiate a bounded alternative.
Language guidance: A token is not a word. Input size, reserved output, and total context are different quantities. The numerical limits in this case are fictional configuration facts, not specifications for a named model.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You are proposing a larger output allowance, correct? / The request fails before generation because the combined reservation is too large. / Twenty-nine thousand plus four thousand is thirty-three thousand. / I see the aim, but that change would increase the reservation. / The allowance is a maximum, not the length already generated. / We must retain the exception clauses in the source context. / Could we remove exact duplicates before shortening substantive text? / A smaller output allowance is acceptable only if the summary still meets the task. / Are those counts from the tokenizer used by this model? / Fitting within the limit does not prove the answer is complete. / Which information would compression remove? / Please report truncation instead of silently dropping the end. / Let us compare the same documents under both configurations. / If the exceptions cannot fit, route the request for a different workflow. / The larger-context model needs its own quality check. / We will remove duplicates, recount, and test coverage before changing models.
Vocabulary: LLM: Large language model; a model that processes and generates language. / transformer: A neural architecture built around attention mechanisms. / token: A unit processed by a model, not necessarily a whole word. / tokenizer: The component that converts text into tokens. / context window: The amount of context a model can handle in a request. / input tokens: Tokens supplied to the model before generation. / output tokens: Tokens generated by the model. / token budget: An allocated number of tokens for a task or component. / system instruction: Higher-priority guidance supplied by an application's configuration. / prompt: Instructions and other context supplied for a model response. / attention: A mechanism that weights relationships between representations. / parameter: A learned numerical value within a model. / weight: A learned numerical coefficient in a model. / checkpoint: Saved model state from a particular stage. / truncation: Removal of content to fit a size limit. / compression: Reduction of information size, potentially losing detail. / reserved capacity: Capacity set aside rather than already consumed. / delimiter: A marker separating sections of supplied text. / instruction hierarchy: Priority relationships among different instruction sources. / long-context: Able to handle relatively large input context. / position: A token's place within the input sequence. / exception clause: Wording that limits or changes a general rule. / deduplication: Removal of repeated copies of content. / headroom: Unused capacity left below a limit.
Speaking task: After checking the dialogue, read the reservation calculation aloud: 29,000 input plus 4,000 reserved output equals 33,000 against a 32,000 limit. Switch roles. Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Data, Datasets, Labels, and LeakageA 94 percent score with an overlap problem+

Your goal

Disclose compromised evidence and resist pressure to overstate readiness.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A 94 percent score with an overlap problem

A classifier scores 94 percent on 500 evaluation examples. Jules finds that 40 evaluation records have exact copies in training. The remaining records have not been re-scored separately. Morgan presents release evidence in one hour. Training and evaluation labels use the same written rubric, but six disputed labels remain unresolved. The release owner can postpone the decision.

Jules: Data scientist / Morgan: Evaluation lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does 94 percent currently describe?

Answer and explanation

C. The score on all 500 evaluation examples

The reported score covers the original 500 examples; the nonoverlapping subset has not been separately scored.

2. What overlap has been confirmed?

Answer and explanation

B. Forty evaluation records have exact training copies.

The confirmed issue is exact overlap for 40 evaluation records, not contamination of every record.

3. What can the release owner do?

Answer and explanation

B. Postpone the decision

The briefing explicitly allows postponement; none of the other actions creates the missing evidence.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A clean holdout is separate from the process it evaluates, not necessarily representative of production. Removing overlap improves separation but does not resolve label disputes or distribution differences.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
dataset
A collection of records assembled for a purpose.curate a dataset
training set
Data used to fit model parameters.audit the training set
validation set
Data used to guide model selection or tuning.monitor validation performance
test set
Data reserved for evaluating a finalized choice.preserve the test set
holdout
Data kept separate from a fitting or selection process.maintain a clean holdout
data leakage
Information crossing a boundary in a way that invalidates evaluation.investigate data leakage
contamination
Unwanted overlap or exposure that weakens an evaluation.quantify evaluation contamination
exact duplicate
A record identical under a stated comparison rule.remove exact duplicates
near duplicate
A closely similar record that may share substantive content.review near duplicates
split
A partition of data into separate subsets.define a reproducible split
label
A target category or annotation attached to a record.verify a disputed label
annotation
Human or machine-added information about an example.review annotation quality
rubric
Explicit criteria for assigning or evaluating labels.apply a shared rubric
adjudication
Resolution of a disputed judgment by a defined process.request label adjudication
inter-rater agreement
The degree to which different raters agree.measure inter-rater agreement
provenance
The recorded origin and history of data.establish data provenance
sampling frame
The population or list from which records are sampled.define the sampling frame
representativeness
How well a sample reflects the target population.assess sample representativeness
class imbalance
Unequal frequencies among target categories.check class imbalance
stratification
Sampling or partitioning within defined groups.use stratified sampling
distribution shift
A difference between data distributions across settings.detect distribution shift
deduplicate
Remove repeated records under a defined rule.deduplicate before splitting
denominator
The total count used as the base of a rate.state the denominator
re-score
Calculate a score again under specified conditions.re-score the clean subset

Exact duplicates and near duplicates require different comparison rules. Inter-rater agreement measures agreement, not automatic correctness. Always identify the sample and denominator behind a percentage.

C Notice the language

Report evidence quality before headline performance

Flagging contaminated evaluation data is responsible reporting, not an admission that every model result is worthless. State exactly what is affected, what cannot yet be concluded, and which decision needs a revised evidence base.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Flag compromised evidence
The headline score includes records that overlap with training.
Quantify the issue
We found 40 exact overlaps among 500 evaluation examples.
Protect uncertainty
We have not yet calculated the score on the remaining records.
Reject unsupported subtraction
Removing eight percent of records does not tell us the new accuracy.
Separate two problems
Data overlap and disputed labels need different checks.
Request provenance
Can we trace when these records entered each split?
Name the decision impact
The current score should not be presented as an independent estimate.
Offer a bounded update
I can provide the overlap list before the meeting.
Clarify a duplicate
What comparison rule defines an exact match here?
Preserve the holdout
Do not tune against the replacement test set.
Qualify a clean result
A clean split still needs a representativeness check.
Check the rubric
Which label criterion produced the disagreement?
State the denominator
Please report both the number correct and the number evaluated.
Describe a limitation
This sample underrepresents the rare class.
Keep the record
Retain the original score with its limitation and the corrected result.
Escalate a decision
The release owner needs to decide whether to wait for the revised evidence.
6 language and precision notes
Includes versus consists of
"The set includes overlapping records" does not mean every record overlaps.
Not yet
"Not yet re-scored" describes incomplete work; it does not imply the corrected result will improve.
Percentage points
A change from 94 percent to 90 percent is four percentage points, not necessarily a four-percent relative decline.
Reported speech
"The slide says the holdout is independent" identifies a claim without endorsing it.
Known and unknown
Pair "we found 40 overlaps" with "we have not checked near duplicates" when both are relevant.
Accountable correction
"We need to correct the slide" directs an action without assigning unsupported personal blame.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which meeting statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. The 94 percent score includes 40 training overlaps.

The overlap is confirmed; no clean-subset score or production outcome has been established.

2. What does "not yet re-scored" mean?

Answer and explanation

D. The revised calculation remains unfinished.

Not yet states timing and incompleteness, not the direction or final consequence of the result.

3. Which question targets a label dispute?

Answer and explanation

A. Which rubric criterion distinguishes these two labels?

The rubric provides criteria for adjudication. Predictions, duplicate removal and an aggregate score do not resolve what the reference category should be.

4. Which phrase correctly names the base of a rate?

Answer and explanation

A. state the denominator

The denominator is the total count against which the number of successes is expressed.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A 94 percent score with an overlap problem

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jules: Data scientist / Morgan: Evaluation lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  • limitation statement
  • provenance

  • representativeness
  • re-score
  • holdout
  • model selection

  1. Morgan
    The release meeting starts in an hour. I have the 94 percent result on the slide. Is there anything material we need to add?
  2. Jules
    Yes. I found 40 exact training matches inside the 500-example evaluation set. That creates 1, so the independence claim needs correcting.
  3. Morgan
    Forty out of 500 is eight percent. Could I subtract eight points and present 86 percent as a conservative estimate instead?
  4. Jules
    No. We need to 2 the remaining examples. We do not know how many of the overlapping records were answered correctly.
  5. Morgan
    Then I will remove the independent-evaluation label. Can you tell me whether the overlap came from the import process or the later cleanup?
  6. Jules
    Not yet. The 3 log should help, but I have not reconstructed the sequence. I can provide the exact record list now.
  7. Morgan
    Send me that list. I'll leave the cause out of the slide for now. Is there anything else I need to flag?
  8. Jules
    Six examples have disputed labels. They need 4 under the written rubric, independently of the overlap check.
  9. Morgan
    Could we exclude those six for this meeting? I do not want the discussion to get buried in detail that may not change the decision.
  10. Jules
    We can report an explicitly defined subset, but we must state its 5 and exclusions. We cannot call a selectively cleaned subset the original result.
  11. Morgan
    Agreed. The release owner can wait for better evidence. What would you recommend we say about the 94 percent figure already circulated yesterday?
  12. Jules
    Retain it with a correction: it describes the original set, not a clean 6. Replace the readiness claim rather than quietly changing the number.
  13. Morgan
    Once the duplicate records are removed, can we say the evaluation represents customer traffic? That is the next question I expect from the release owner.
  14. Jules
    Not from separation alone. We still need to check 7, including the rare category that appears only a few times in this sample.
  15. Morgan
    So we have three issues: overlap, disputed labels, and coverage of the target population. Which one can you resolve before today's meeting starts?
  16. Jules
    I can verify the 8 and document the matching rule. I cannot responsibly promise all label reviews within the hour.
  17. Morgan
    All right, the duplicate check comes first. For the replacement set, can we keep it untouched while the team tunes the model?
  18. Jules
    It should. If we repeatedly choose model settings from that set, it becomes part of 9, and we need a separate final test.
  19. Morgan
    I will recommend postponing the readiness decision. The slide will list confirmed overlap, pending label review, and the missing clean-subset score separately.
  20. Jules
    I will send the records and a short 10. That gives the owner usable evidence without pretending the corrected outcome is already known.

Answers and explanations
  1. contamination Turn 2. Contamination means the evaluation is affected by unwanted exposure or overlap with training data.
  2. re-score Turn 4. Re-scoring calculates performance on the specified remaining records; arithmetic on the overlap rate cannot supply that score.
  3. provenance Turn 6. Provenance records where data came from and how it changed; it can help identify when overlap entered the sets.
  4. adjudication Turn 8. Adjudication resolves disputed labels through the agreed review process rather than selecting whichever label improves the score.
  5. denominator Turn 10. The denominator identifies how many records are included, so exclusions cannot be hidden behind an unchanged headline rate.
  6. holdout Turn 12. A holdout must remain separate from the process being evaluated; the confirmed training overlap compromises that separation.
  7. representativeness Turn 14. Representativeness concerns whether the sample reflects the intended customer population, a separate question from overlap.
  8. exact duplicates Turn 16. Exact duplicates are identical under the chosen comparison rule; verifying them does not complete a near-duplicate review.
  9. model selection Turn 18. Model selection uses evaluation results to choose a model or configuration, so that set no longer provides an untouched final test.
  10. limitation statement Turn 20. A limitation statement specifies what the evidence cannot support, preventing the headline figure from becoming an unjustified release claim.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: The release meeting starts in an hour. I have the 94 percent result on the slide. Is there anything material we need to add?
  2. Jules: Yes. I found 40 exact training matches inside the 500-example evaluation set. That creates contamination, so the independence claim needs correcting.
  3. Morgan: Forty out of 500 is eight percent. Could I subtract eight points and present 86 percent as a conservative estimate instead?
  4. Jules: No. We need to re-score the remaining examples. We do not know how many of the overlapping records were answered correctly.

  6. Jules: Not yet. The provenance log should help, but I have not reconstructed the sequence. I can provide the exact record list now.
  7. Morgan: Send me that list. I'll leave the cause out of the slide for now. Is there anything else I need to flag?
  8. Jules: Six examples have disputed labels. They need adjudication under the written rubric, independently of the overlap check.
  9. Morgan: Could we exclude those six for this meeting? I do not want the discussion to get buried in detail that may not change the decision.
  10. Jules: We can report an explicitly defined subset, but we must state its denominator and exclusions. We cannot call a selectively cleaned subset the original result.
  11. Morgan: Agreed. The release owner can wait for better evidence. What would you recommend we say about the 94 percent figure already circulated yesterday?
  12. Jules: Retain it with a correction: it describes the original set, not a clean holdout. Replace the readiness claim rather than quietly changing the number.
  13. Morgan: Once the duplicate records are removed, can we say the evaluation represents customer traffic? That is the next question I expect from the release owner.
  14. Jules: Not from separation alone. We still need to check representativeness, including the rare category that appears only a few times in this sample.
  15. Morgan: So we have three issues: overlap, disputed labels, and coverage of the target population. Which one can you resolve before today's meeting starts?
